Algebra 1 Unit 7 Bank

Exponential Functions and Modeling

7.1 Recognizing Growth and Decay

Students will identify exponential relationships from patterns, tables, graphs, and situations.

WTC

U7_S1_WTC_01WTCTable
Target: I can construct evidence from patterns, tables, graphs, and situations to support an exponential growth or decay relationship.
Evidence job: Preview the section intellectual territory without requiring an untaught formal procedure.

Two quantities start at the same value. Study the shared table. Without naming a formal rule yet, describe how each column changes, predict the next value in each column, and explain which column is better described by repeated multiplication.

stepAB
01212
11718
22227
32740.5
Answer / verification

A adds 5 each step, so its next value is 32. B multiplies by 1.5 each step, so its next value is 60.75. Column B is the repeated-multiplication pattern.

Notes Example

U7_S1_NOTES_EXAMPLE_01Notes ExampleTable
Target: I can construct evidence from patterns, tables, graphs, and situations to support an exponential growth or decay relationship.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Use the table to decide whether the relationship is exponential growth or exponential decay. State the constant ratio that supports your decision.

xy
0$\displaystyle 3$
1$\displaystyle 6$
2$\displaystyle 12$
3$\displaystyle 24$
4$\displaystyle 48$
Answer / verification

Growth; each output is multiplied by $\displaystyle 2$ when $x$ increases by 1.

U7_S1_NOTES_EXAMPLE_02Notes ExampleEquation
Target: I can apply repeated multiplication and constant ratios to exponential patterns.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

The sequence is $\displaystyle 6, 9, \frac{27}{2}, \frac{81}{4}, \ldots$ . Find the next two terms and explain whether the pattern shows exponential growth or decay.

Answer / verification

Next terms: $\frac{243}{8}, \frac{729}{16}$. The constant multiplier is $\frac{3}{2}$, so the pattern shows exponential growth.

U7_S1_NOTES_EXAMPLE_03Notes ExampleEquation
Target: I can determine whether a relationship is exponential by checking how values change across representations.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Consider $y=4(\frac{3}{2})^x$. Decide whether the relationship represents exponential growth or decay. Identify the initial value and the growth or decay factor.

Answer / verification

Growth; initial value $\displaystyle 4$ and factor $\frac{3}{2}$.

U7_S1_NOTES_EXAMPLE_04Notes ExampleGraph
Target: I can construct evidence from patterns, tables, graphs, and situations to support an exponential growth or decay relationship.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Use the graph to decide whether the relationship shows exponential growth or decay. Give one graph feature that supports your decision.

Answer / verification

Exponential growth. The curve rises more rapidly as x increases; the change is multiplicative rather than a constant vertical difference.

U7_S1_NOTES_EXAMPLE_05Notes ExampleContext
Target: I can construct evidence from patterns, tables, graphs, and situations to support an exponential growth or decay relationship.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A video is shared to 35% more people each hour. Use the repeated-percent behavior to justify an exponential model and identify the per-period multiplier.

Answer / verification

The situation describes repeated percent change over equal time intervals, so an exponential model is reasonable. The multiplier is $\frac{27}{20}$, which gives growth.

U7_S1_NOTES_EXAMPLE_06Notes ExampleTable + Equation
Target: I can determine whether a relationship is exponential by checking how values change across representations.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

The equation is $y=6(\frac{3}{2})^x$. Use the table to determine whether it represents the same relationship. Give evidence from at least two rows.

xy
0$\displaystyle 6$
1$\displaystyle 9$
2$\frac{27}{2}$
3$\frac{81}{4}$
Answer / verification

Yes. Substituting the listed $x$-values into $y=6(\frac{3}{2})^x$ gives the table outputs; the table also has constant ratio $\frac{3}{2}$.

U7_S1_NOTES_YTI_03Notes YTIEquation

Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

U7_S1_NOTES_YTI_04Notes YTIGraph
Target: I can construct evidence from patterns, tables, graphs, and situations to support an exponential growth or decay relationship.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Use the graph to decide whether the relationship shows exponential growth or decay. Give one graph feature that supports your decision.

Answer / verification

Exponential decay. The curve decreases toward the horizontal axis as x increases; the change is multiplicative rather than a constant vertical difference.

U7_S1_NOTES_YTI_05Notes YTIContext
Target: I can construct evidence from patterns, tables, graphs, and situations to support an exponential growth or decay relationship.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A laptop keeps 82% of its value each year. State the multiplier and connect it to why a single exponential model is appropriate.

Answer / verification

The situation describes repeated percent change over equal time intervals, so an exponential model is reasonable. The multiplier is $\frac{41}{50}$, which gives decay.

U7_S1_PRACTICE_SET_1_03Practice Set 1Context
Target: I can apply repeated multiplication and constant ratios to exponential patterns.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A quantity increases by 6% each period. Write the multiplicative factor used from one period to the next.

Answer / verification

The factor is $\frac{53}{50}$ (1.06).

U7_S1_PRACTICE_SET_1_04Practice Set 1Equation
Target: I can apply repeated multiplication and constant ratios to exponential patterns.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

An exponential relationship uses a multiplier of $\frac{3}{5}$ for each increase of 1 in $x$. Describe the percent change and tell whether it is growth or decay.

Answer / verification

40% decrease (decay).

U7_S1_PRACTICE_SET_1_18Practice Set 1Context
Target: I can construct evidence from patterns, tables, graphs, and situations to support an exponential growth or decay relationship.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

The newsletter subscribers increases by 12% each week. Use the repeated-percent behavior to justify an exponential model and identify the per-week multiplier.

Answer / verification

The situation describes repeated percent change over equal week intervals, so an exponential model is reasonable. The multiplier is $\frac{28}{25}$, which gives growth.

U7_S1_PRACTICE_SET_1_19Practice Set 1Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations using the quadratic formula and interpret solutions.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Solve $\displaystyle x^{2} + 3 x - 1=0$ using the quadratic formula. Give exact solutions.

Answer / verification

$x=- \frac{3}{2} + \frac{\sqrt{13}}{2}$ or $x=- \frac{\sqrt{13}}{2} - \frac{3}{2}$.

U7_S1_PRACTICE_SET_1_20Practice Set 1Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations using the quadratic formula and interpret solutions.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

For $\displaystyle x^{2} - 6 x + 5=0$, compute the discriminant and use it to state how many real solutions the equation has.

Answer / verification

The discriminant is $\displaystyle 16$, so the equation has two real solutions.

U7_S1_PRACTICE_SET_1_21Practice Set 1Context + Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations using the quadratic formula and interpret solutions.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A height model leads to the equation $\displaystyle 2 t^{2} - 14 t - 5=0$ for possible times when an object is at ground level, where $t$ is measured in seconds and $t\ge 0$. Use the quadratic formula and interpret the meaningful solution.

Answer / verification

The positive solution is $t\approx 7.34$ seconds. The negative solution is outside the contextual domain, so the meaningful time is about 7.34 seconds.

U7_S1_PRACTICE_SET_1_22Practice Set 1Equation
Target: Recognize and factor special polynomial patterns.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Factor $\displaystyle 25x^2-4$ completely.

Answer / verification

$(5x-2)(5x+2)$.

U7_S1_PRACTICE_SET_1_23Practice Set 1Equation
Target: Recognize and factor special polynomial patterns.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Factor $x^{2} + 6 x + 9$ completely.

Answer / verification

$(x+3)^2$.

U7_S1_PRACTICE_SET_1_24Practice Set 1Equation
Target: Recognize and factor special polynomial patterns.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Identify the special factoring pattern in $x^{2} + 8 x + 16$ and factor the expression.

Answer / verification

Perfect-Square Trinomial; $(x+4)^2$.

U7_S1_PRACTICE_SET_2_21Practice Set 2Context + Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations using the quadratic formula and interpret solutions.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A model leads to the equation $\displaystyle 3t^2-14t-5=0$, where only a positive value of $t$ is meaningful. Use the quadratic formula and interpret the meaningful solution.

Answer / verification

The positive solution is $t\approx 5.00$. The negative solution is not meaningful for the stated context, so the model answer is about 5.00 units of distance.

Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Use the graph to decide whether the relationship shows exponential growth or decay. Give one graph feature that supports your decision.

Answer / verification

Exponential decay. The curve decreases toward the horizontal axis as x increases; the change is multiplicative rather than a constant vertical difference.

Answer / verification

The positive solution is $t\approx 3.00$ seconds. The negative solution is outside the contextual domain, so the meaningful time is about 3.00 seconds.

Exit Ticket B

U7_S1_EXIT_TICKET_B_01Exit Ticket BEquation
Target: Solve quadratic equations using the quadratic formula and interpret solutions.
Evidence job: Quick evidence for Unit 6 quadratic review retrieval embedded in Unit 7 section 1.

Solve $\displaystyle 2 x^{2} + 3 x - 3=0$ using the quadratic formula. Give exact solutions.

Answer / verification

$x=- \frac{3}{4} + \frac{\sqrt{33}}{4}$ or $x=- \frac{\sqrt{33}}{4} - \frac{3}{4}$.

U7_S1_EXIT_TICKET_B_02Exit Ticket BEquation
Target: Solve quadratic equations using the quadratic formula and interpret solutions.
Evidence job: Quick evidence for Unit 6 quadratic review retrieval embedded in Unit 7 section 1.

For $\displaystyle 3 x^{2} - 6 x + 3=0$, compute the discriminant and use it to state how many real solutions the equation has.

Answer / verification

The discriminant is $\displaystyle 0$, so the equation has one real solution.

U7_S1_EXIT_TICKET_B_03Exit Ticket BContext + Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations using the quadratic formula and interpret solutions.
Evidence job: Quick evidence for Unit 6 quadratic review retrieval embedded in Unit 7 section 1.

A height model leads to the equation $\displaystyle 4 t^{2} - 16 t - 7=0$ for possible times when an object is at ground level, where $t$ is measured in seconds and $t\ge 0$. Use the quadratic formula and interpret the meaningful solution.

Answer / verification

The positive solution is $t\approx 4.40$ seconds. The negative solution is outside the contextual domain, so the meaningful time is about 4.40 seconds.

U7_S1_EXIT_TICKET_B_04Exit Ticket BEquation
Target: Solve quadratic equations using the quadratic formula and interpret solutions.
Evidence job: Quick evidence for Unit 6 quadratic review retrieval embedded in Unit 7 section 1.

Solve $\displaystyle x^2-4x+1=0$ using the quadratic formula. Give exact solutions.

Answer / verification

$x=2\pm\sqrt{3}$.

$(4x-5)(4x+5)$.

U7_S1_EXIT_TICKET_C_02Exit Ticket CEquation

U7_S1_EXIT_TICKET_C_03Exit Ticket CEquation
Target: Recognize and factor special polynomial patterns.
Evidence job: Portfolio evidence for U7-MG02: Factor quadratic trinomials and special polynomial patterns by using product-sum reasoning and recognizable structure.

Identify the special factoring pattern in $x^{2} - 12 x + 36$ and factor the expression.

Answer / verification

Perfect-Square Trinomial; $(x-6)^2$.

U7_S1_EXIT_TICKET_C_04Exit Ticket CEquation
Target: Recognize and factor special polynomial patterns.
Evidence job: Portfolio evidence for U7-MG02: Factor quadratic trinomials and special polynomial patterns by using product-sum reasoning and recognizable structure.

Answer / verification

$(5x-7)(5x+7)$.

7.2 Writing Exponential Equations

Students will write exponential equations from tables, graphs, and situations.

WTC

U7_S2_WTC_01WTCTable + Equation
Target: I can build exponential equations using an initial value and a growth or decay factor.
Evidence job: Preview the section intellectual territory without requiring an untaught formal procedure.

A quantity follows the shared table. Identify what seems to stay consistent from row to row, identify the starting value, and propose one equation that would continue the pattern. Explain what each number in your equation represents.

xy
08
112
218
327
Answer / verification

The starting value is 8 and the constant factor is 1.5, so a matching equation is $y=8(1.5)^x$. The 8 is the value at $x=0$ and 1.5 is the per-step multiplier.

Notes Example

U7_S2_NOTES_EXAMPLE_01Notes ExampleTable + Equation
Target: I can build exponential equations using an initial value and a growth or decay factor.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Write an exponential equation of the form $y=a(b)^x$ for the table.

xy
0$\displaystyle 3$
1$\displaystyle 6$
2$\displaystyle 12$
3$\displaystyle 24$
Answer / verification

$y=3(2)^x$.

U7_S2_NOTES_EXAMPLE_02Notes ExampleContext + Equation
Target: I can formulate exponential equations that represent repeated percent increase or decrease.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A colony begins with 80 cells and increases by 25% each hour. Write an exponential equation for the amount after $h$ hours.

Answer / verification

$A=80(\frac{5}{4})^h$.

U7_S2_NOTES_EXAMPLE_03Notes ExampleContext + Equation
Target: I can formulate exponential equations that represent repeated percent increase or decrease.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A quantity begins at 800 and decreases by 10% per period. Write a model for its value after $t$ periods.

Answer / verification

$A(t)=800(\frac{9}{10})^t$.

The graph below is consistent with a model whose $y$-intercept is 3. Compare the candidates $y=3(1.5)^x$ and $y=1.5(3)^x$. Which equation fits the graph more directly? Use the $y$-intercept first, then check the multiplicative growth.

Answer / verification

$y=3(1.5)^x$ fits: its initial value is 3, matching the $y$-intercept, and its base is greater than 1 so the graph grows.

U7_S2_PRACTICE_SET_1_07Practice Set 1Equation
Target: I can build exponential equations using an initial value and a growth or decay factor.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

An exponential function has $f(0)=3$ and $f(2)=12$. The factor is positive. Determine the per-unit factor and write the function.

Answer / verification

Because two equal steps multiply by $\displaystyle 4$, the positive per-step factor is $\displaystyle 2$. Thus $f(x)=3(2)^x$.

Answer / verification

The claim is correct; distributing the factor reproduces $\displaystyle 15x^3-10x^2$.

Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A student claims $\displaystyle 9 x^{3} + 12 x^{2}=3x^{2}(3x+4)$. Determine whether the claim is correct. Verify by multiplication and, if needed, give the correct factored form.

Answer / verification

The claim is correct; distributing the factor reproduces $\displaystyle 9 x^{3} + 12 x^{2}$.

Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

For $f(x)=7(\frac{4}{3})^x$, identify the initial value and the growth or decay factor.

Answer / verification

Initial value $\displaystyle 7$; factor $\frac{4}{3}$ (growth).

U7_S2_PRACTICE_SET_3_05Practice Set 3Equation
Target: I can build exponential equations using an initial value and a growth or decay factor.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

An exponential function has $f(0)=6$ and $f(3)=162$. The factor is positive. Determine the per-unit factor and write the function.

Answer / verification

Across 3 equal steps the outputs multiply by $27$, so the positive per-step factor is $3$. Thus $f(x)=6(3)^x$.

U7_S2_PRACTICE_SET_3_06Practice Set 3Graph + Equation
Target: I can build exponential equations using an initial value and a growth or decay factor.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

The graph below is consistent with a model whose $y$-intercept is 1.5. Compare the candidates $y=1.5(1.75)^x$ and $y=1.75(1.5)^x$. Which equation fits the graph more directly? Support your choice with one intercept feature and one growth feature.

Answer / verification

$y=1.5(1.75)^x$ fits: its initial value is 1.5, matching the $y$-intercept, and its base is greater than 1 so the graph grows.

U7_S2_EXIT_TICKET_A_03Exit Ticket AGraph + Equation
Target: I can build exponential equations using an initial value and a growth or decay factor.
Evidence job: Quick evidence for current Unit 7 section 2 Intro learning.

The graph below is consistent with a model whose $y$-intercept is 5.5. Compare the candidates $y=5.5(1.1)^x$ and $y=1.1(5.5)^x$. Which equation fits the graph more directly? Use the $y$-intercept first, then check the multiplicative growth.

Answer / verification

$y=5.5(1.1)^x$ fits: its initial value is 5.5, matching the $y$-intercept, and its base is greater than 1 so the graph grows.

U7_S2_EXIT_TICKET_B_02Exit Ticket BContext + Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations using factored form and interpret solutions.

A quadratic model is $h(t)=(t+4)(t-5)$. Find the zeros. If $t$ represents time, identify which zero(s) could be meaningful when $t\ge 0$.

Answer / verification

Zeros: $\displaystyle -4$ and $\displaystyle 5$. For $t\ge0$, the meaningful zero is 5.

U7_S2_EXIT_TICKET_C_01Exit Ticket CEquation
Target: Identify common factors and use structure to rewrite polynomial expressions.
Evidence job: Portfolio evidence for U7-MG01: Factor polynomial expressions by identifying a greatest common factor and use multiplication to verify equivalent forms.

Factor $\displaystyle 16 x^{5} + 28 x^{3}$ completely by removing the greatest common factor.

Answer / verification

$\displaystyle 4x^{3}(4x^2+7)$.

U7_S2_EXIT_TICKET_C_02Exit Ticket CEquation
Target: Identify common factors and use structure to rewrite polynomial expressions.
Evidence job: Portfolio evidence for U7-MG01: Factor polynomial expressions by identifying a greatest common factor and use multiplication to verify equivalent forms.

Factor $\displaystyle 16 x^{2} + 36 x$ completely using the greatest common factor.

Answer / verification

$\displaystyle 4x(4x+9)$.

U7_S2_EXIT_TICKET_C_03Exit Ticket CEquation
Target: Identify common factors and use structure to rewrite polynomial expressions.
Evidence job: Portfolio evidence for U7-MG01: Factor polynomial expressions by identifying a greatest common factor and use multiplication to verify equivalent forms.

A student claims $\displaystyle 25 x^{3} + 40 x^{2}=5x^{2}(5x+9)$. Determine whether the claim is correct. Verify by multiplication and, if needed, give the correct factored form.

Answer / verification

The claim is not correct. The correct factored form is $\displaystyle 5x^{2}(5x+8)$; distributing reproduces $\displaystyle 25 x^{3} + 40 x^{2}$.

U7_S2_EXIT_TICKET_C_04Exit Ticket CEquation

Evidence job: Portfolio evidence for U7-MG01: Factor polynomial expressions by identifying a greatest common factor and use multiplication to verify equivalent forms.

Factor $\displaystyle 25 x^{6} + 45 x^{4}$ completely by removing the greatest common factor.

Answer / verification

$\displaystyle 5x^{4}(5x^2+9)$.

7.3 Graphing and Analyzing Exponential Functions

Students will graph exponential functions and analyze key features.

WTC

U7_S3_WTC_01WTCGraph
Target: I can design graphs of exponential functions using key points, growth or decay factors, and asymptote behavior.
Evidence job: Preview the section intellectual territory without requiring an untaught formal procedure.

Compare the two shared graphs. For each graph, notice the intercept, the direction of change, and what happens near the horizontal axis. List two features the graphs share and one important way they differ.

Answer / verification

Both have positive $y$-intercepts, remain above the horizontal axis, and approach $y=0$ in one direction. One rises as $x$ increases while the other falls as $x$ increases.

Notes Example

U7_S3_NOTES_EXAMPLE_01Notes ExampleEquation + Table
Target: I can design graphs of exponential functions using key points, growth or decay factors, and asymptote behavior.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

For $f(x)=2(2)^x$, complete a key-point table for $x=-1,0,1,2$. Use the points to describe the graph as growth or decay.

Answer / verification
xf(x)
-1$\displaystyle 1$
0$\displaystyle 2$
1$\displaystyle 4$
2$\displaystyle 8$

The graph shows growth.

U7_S3_NOTES_EXAMPLE_02Notes ExampleEquation + Graph
Target: I can design graphs of exponential functions using key points, growth or decay factors, and asymptote behavior.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Make a small table of key points and sketch $y=3(\frac{3}{2})^x$. Label the $y$-intercept and horizontal asymptote.

Answer / verification

Useful points include $(-1,2)$, $(0,3)$, $(1,\frac{9}{2})$, $(2,\frac{27}{4})$. The $y$-intercept is $(0,3)$ and the horizontal asymptote is $y=0$.

U7_S3_NOTES_EXAMPLE_03Notes ExampleEquation
Target: I can apply key features of exponential graphs to interpret domain, range, intercepts, and end behavior.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

State the domain and range of $f(x)=8(\frac{1}{2})^x$.

Answer / verification

Domain: all real numbers. Range: $y\gt 0$.

U7_S3_NOTES_EXAMPLE_04Notes ExampleGraph
Target: I can apply key features of exponential graphs to interpret domain, range, intercepts, and end behavior.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Use the graph to identify the $y$-intercept, describe the end behavior, and decide whether it shows exponential growth or decay.

Answer / verification

The graph has a positive $y$-intercept, approaches $y=0$ to the left, and rises rapidly to the right; it shows exponential growth.

U7_S3_NOTES_EXAMPLE_05Notes ExampleGraph
Target: I can design graphs of exponential functions using key points, growth or decay factors, and asymptote behavior.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Graphs A and B have the same positive initial value, but one uses factor 1.5 and the other uses factor 2. Which graph grows faster for positive $x$? Explain how the graph supports your answer.

Answer / verification

The graph with factor 2 grows faster. It rises more steeply and its outputs separate increasingly from the factor-1.5 graph as $x$ increases.

U7_S3_NOTES_EXAMPLE_06Notes ExampleEquation
Target: I can apply key features of exponential graphs to interpret domain, range, intercepts, and end behavior.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Describe what happens to $f(x)=7(\frac{1}{2})^x$ as $x$ becomes very large and as $x$ becomes very negative.

Answer / verification

As $x\to\infty$, $f(x)$ approaches 0 from above; as $x\to-\infty$, $f(x)$ grows without bound.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_1_19Practice Set 1Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations by completing the square and connect the process to vertex form.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Solve $x^{2} + 4 x - 5=0$ by completing the square.

Answer / verification

$(x+2)^2=9$, so $x=1$ or $x=-5$.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_1_20Practice Set 1Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations by completing the square and connect the process to vertex form.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Rewrite $y=x^{2} + 4 x - 3$ in vertex form by completing the square, then state the vertex.

Answer / verification

$y=(x+2)^2-7$. The vertex is $(-2,-7)$.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_1_21Practice Set 1Equation

Answer / verification

$x=0$ or $x=-6$.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_1_23Practice Set 1Equation

Answer / verification

$f(x)=(x+2)(x-5)$; intercepts $(-2,0)$ and $(5,0)$.

Answer / verification

A possible function is $f(x)=(x+3)(x-2)$. Each factor is zero at one $x$-intercept.

Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Use the graph to identify the $y$-intercept, describe the end behavior, and decide whether it shows exponential growth or decay.

Answer / verification

The graph has a positive $y$-intercept, decreases toward $y=0$ to the right, and grows to the left; it shows exponential decay.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_2_16Practice Set 2Equation + Graph description
Target: I can assess whether a graph is reasonable for an exponential equation or situation.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A student graphs $y=5(0.6)^x$ and draws the curve crossing the x-axis at $x=4$. Decide whether it is reasonable, citing the intercept/asymptote behavior that matters.

Answer / verification

Not reasonable. A basic positive exponential graph has horizontal asymptote $y=0$ and does not cross it.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_2_17Practice Set 2Graph
Target: I can design graphs of exponential functions using key points, growth or decay factors, and asymptote behavior.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Graphs A and B start at the same positive value. Which graph will have the larger output at $x=3$? Use the factors shown by the graph family to justify your choice.

Answer / verification

Graph B. Its factor is 2, so its output at $x=3$ is 8, while Graph A with factor 1.5 has output 3.375.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_2_18Practice Set 2Equation
Target: I can apply key features of exponential graphs to interpret domain, range, intercepts, and end behavior.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Describe what happens to $f(x)=6(\frac{3}{2})^x$ as $x$ becomes very large and as $x$ becomes very negative.

Answer / verification

As $x\to\infty$, $f(x)$ grows without bound; as $x\to-\infty$, $f(x)$ approaches 0 from above.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_2_19Practice Set 2Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations by completing the square and connect the process to vertex form.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Solve $x^{2} + 6 x - 16=0$ by completing the square.

Answer / verification

$(x+3)^2=25$, so $x=2$ or $x=-8$.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_2_20Practice Set 2Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations by completing the square and connect the process to vertex form.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Rewrite $y=x^2+4x-3$ in vertex form by completing the square, then state the vertex.

Answer / verification

$y=(x+2)^2-7$. The vertex is $(-2,-7)$.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_2_21Practice Set 2Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations by completing the square and connect the process to vertex form.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Solve $(x-1)^2=4$ by using the square-root step that appears after completing the square.

Answer / verification

$x=3$ or $x=-1$.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_2_22Practice Set 2Equation
Target: Connect factors, zeros, intercepts, and graphs of quadratic functions.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

For $f(x)=(x+3)(x-2)$, find the zeros and state the corresponding $x$-intercepts.

Answer / verification

Zeros: $x=-3$ and $x=2$; $x$-intercepts: $(-3,0)$ and $(2,0)$.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_2_23Practice Set 2Equation
Target: Connect factors, zeros, intercepts, and graphs of quadratic functions.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A monic quadratic has zeros $x=3$ and $x=6$. Write the function in factored form and identify its $x$-intercepts.

Answer / verification

$f(x)=(x-3)(x-6)$; intercepts $(3,0)$ and $(6,0)$.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_2_24Practice Set 2Graph + Equation
Target: Connect factors, zeros, intercepts, and graphs of quadratic functions.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

The graph crosses the $x$-axis at $x=-4$ and $x=1$. Write a possible monic quadratic in factored form and explain how the factors encode the intercepts.

Answer / verification

A possible function is $f(x)=(x+4)(x-1)$. Each factor is zero at one $x$-intercept.

Practice Set 3

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_3_01Practice Set 3Equation
Target: I can apply key features of exponential graphs to interpret domain, range, intercepts, and end behavior.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Without graphing every point, identify the $y$-intercept of $f(x)=11(\frac{4}{3})^x$ and explain how you know.

Answer / verification

At $x=0$, the exponential factor is 1, so $f(0)=11$. The $y$-intercept is $(0,11)$.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_3_02Practice Set 3Equation
Target: I can apply key features of exponential graphs to interpret domain, range, intercepts, and end behavior.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

For the basic exponential function $y=8(\frac{4}{3})^x$, state the horizontal asymptote and describe whether the graph reaches it.

Answer / verification

The horizontal asymptote is $y=0$. The graph approaches the asymptote but does not reach or cross it.

Answer / verification

Domain: all real numbers. Range: $y\gt0$.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_3_06Practice Set 3Graph
Target: I can design graphs of exponential functions using key points, growth or decay factors, and asymptote behavior.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Graphs A and B have the same positive initial value, but one uses factor 1.5 and the other uses factor 2. Which graph grows faster for positive $x$? Explain how the graph supports your answer.

Answer / verification

The graph with factor 2 grows faster. It rises more steeply and its outputs separate increasingly from the factor-1.5 graph as $x$ increases.

Answer / verification

The model starts at 30 when $t=0$ and grows increasingly rapidly as hours pass.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_3_10Practice Set 3Equation + Graph
Target: I can design graphs of exponential functions using key points, growth or decay factors, and asymptote behavior.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Answer / verification

Useful points include $(-1,3)$, $(0,5)$, $(1,\frac{25}{3})$, $(2,\frac{125}{9})$. The $y$-intercept is $(0,5)$ and the horizontal asymptote is $y=0$.

U7_S3_PRACTICE_SET_3_16Practice Set 3Graph

Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Use the two exponential graphs to explain why sharing the same $y$-intercept does not mean two functions have the same growth rate.

Answer / verification

Both begin at the same initial value, but Graph B uses factor 2 while Graph A uses factor 1.5. Their outputs separate as $x$ increases, so the growth rates differ.

Warm-Up 1

U7_S3_WARM_UP_1_01Warm-Up 1Diamond diagram
Target: Solve quadratic equations by completing the square and connect the process to vertex form.
Evidence job: Retrieve prior product-sum structure used in quadratic work.

Complete the Diamond Problem: find two integers whose product is 20 and whose sum is 9.

Answer / verification

The integers are 4 and 5.

U7_S3_WARM_UP_1_02Warm-Up 1Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations by completing the square and connect the process to vertex form.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Solve $x^2+6x+5=0$ by completing the square.

Answer / verification

$(x+3)^2=4$, so $x=-1$ or $x=-5$.

U7_S3_WARM_UP_1_03Warm-Up 1Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations by completing the square and connect the process to vertex form.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Rewrite $y=x^2+6x+2$ in vertex form by completing the square, then state the vertex.

Answer / verification

$y=(x+3)^2-7$. The vertex is $(-3,-7)$.

U7_S3_WARM_UP_1_04Warm-Up 1Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations by completing the square and connect the process to vertex form.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Solve $(x+3)^2=16$ by using the square-root step that appears after completing the square.

Answer / verification

$x=1$ or $x=-7$.

Warm-Up 2

U7_S3_WARM_UP_2_01Warm-Up 2Equation + Table
Target: I can design graphs of exponential functions using key points, growth or decay factors, and asymptote behavior.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

For $f(x)=8(\frac{4}{5})^x$, complete a key-point table for $x=-1,0,1,2$. Use the points to describe the graph as growth or decay.

Answer / verification
xf(x)
-1$\displaystyle 10$
0$\displaystyle 8$
1$\displaystyle \frac{32}{5}$
2$\displaystyle \frac{128}{25}$

The graph shows decay.

U7_S3_WARM_UP_2_02Warm-Up 2Equation + Graph
Target: I can design graphs of exponential functions using key points, growth or decay factors, and asymptote behavior.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Make a small table of key points and sketch $y=2(\frac{3}{4})^x$. Label the $y$-intercept and horizontal asymptote.

Answer / verification

Useful points include $(-1,\frac{8}{3})$, $(0,2)$, $(1,\frac{3}{2})$, $(2,\frac{9}{8})$. The $y$-intercept is $(0,2)$ and the horizontal asymptote is $y=0$.

U7_S3_WARM_UP_2_03Warm-Up 2Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations by completing the square and connect the process to vertex form.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Solve $x^2+8x+7=0$ by completing the square.

Answer / verification

$(x+4)^2=9$, so $x=-1$ or $x=-7$.

U7_S3_WARM_UP_2_04Warm-Up 2Equation
Target: Connect factors, zeros, intercepts, and graphs of quadratic functions.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

For $f(x)=(x+4)(x+1)$, find the zeros and state the corresponding $x$-intercepts.

Answer / verification

Zeros: $x=-4$ and $x=-1$; $x$-intercepts: $(-4,0)$ and $(-1,0)$.

Warm-Up 3

U7_S3_WARM_UP_3_01Warm-Up 3Equation
Target: I can apply key features of exponential graphs to interpret domain, range, intercepts, and end behavior.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Without graphing every point, identify the $y$-intercept of $f(x)=12(\frac{3}{4})^x$ and explain how you know.

Answer / verification

At $x=0$, the exponential factor is 1, so $f(0)=12$. The $y$-intercept is $(0,12)$.

U7_S3_WARM_UP_3_02Warm-Up 3Equation
Target: Solve quadratic equations by completing the square and connect the process to vertex form.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Rewrite $y=x^{2} + 8 x + 11$ in vertex form by completing the square, then state the vertex.

Answer / verification

$y=(x+4)^2-5$. The vertex is $(-4,-5)$.

U7_S3_WARM_UP_3_03Warm-Up 3Equation
Target: Connect factors, zeros, intercepts, and graphs of quadratic functions.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A monic quadratic has zeros $x=-2$ and $x=4$. Write the function in factored form and identify its $x$-intercepts.

Answer / verification

$f(x)=(x+2)(x-4)$; intercepts $(-2,0)$ and $(4,0)$.

U7_S3_WARM_UP_3_04Warm-Up 3Graph + Equation
Target: Connect factors, zeros, intercepts, and graphs of quadratic functions.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

The graph crosses the $x$-axis at $x=-1$ and $x=4$. Write a possible monic quadratic in factored form and explain how the factors encode the intercepts.

Answer / verification

A possible function is $f(x)=(x+1)(x-4)$. Each factor is zero at one $x$-intercept.

Exit Ticket A

U7_S3_EXIT_TICKET_A_01Exit Ticket AEquation + Graph
Target: I can design graphs of exponential functions using key points, growth or decay factors, and asymptote behavior.
Evidence job: Quick evidence for current Unit 7 section 3 Intro learning.

Make a small table of key points and sketch $y=5(\frac{2}{3})^x$. Label the $y$-intercept and horizontal asymptote.

Answer / verification

Useful points include $(-1,\frac{15}{2})$, $(0,5)$, $(1,\frac{10}{3})$, $(2,\frac{20}{9})$. The $y$-intercept is $(0,5)$ and the horizontal asymptote is $y=0$.

U7_S3_EXIT_TICKET_A_02Exit Ticket AEquation
Target: I can apply key features of exponential graphs to interpret domain, range, intercepts, and end behavior.
Evidence job: Quick evidence for current Unit 7 section 3 Intro learning.

State the domain and range of $f(x)=14(\frac{3}{4})^x$.

Answer / verification

Domain: all real numbers. Range: $y\gt0$.

U7_S3_EXIT_TICKET_A_03Exit Ticket AGraph
Target: I can design graphs of exponential functions using key points, growth or decay factors, and asymptote behavior.
Evidence job: Quick evidence for current Unit 7 section 3 Intro learning.

Graphs A and B have the same positive initial value, but one uses factor 1.5 and the other uses factor 2. Which graph grows faster for positive $x$? Explain how the graph supports your answer.

Answer / verification

The graph with factor 2 grows faster. It rises more steeply and its outputs separate increasingly from the factor-1.5 graph as $x$ increases.

U7_S3_EXIT_TICKET_A_04Exit Ticket AGraph + Equation
Target: I can assess whether a graph is reasonable for an exponential equation or situation.
Evidence job: Quick evidence for current Unit 7 section 3 Intro learning.

A student claims the graph below represents $y=6(0.7)^x$. Assess the claim using the $y$-intercept, growth/decay direction, and asymptote behavior.

Answer / verification

The claim is not reasonable. The equation has initial value 6 and a factor between 0 and 1, so it should show decay toward the horizontal asymptote $y=0$. The displayed graph grows as $x$ increases.

7.4 Comparing Function Families

Students will compare linear, quadratic, and exponential functions using multiple representations.

WTC

U7_S4_WTC_01WTCTable
Target: I can formulate comparisons among linear, quadratic, and exponential functions using rates, differences, ratios, and graph features.
Evidence job: Preview the section intellectual territory without requiring an untaught formal procedure.

Three columns come from three different function families. Use the shared table to describe how each column changes. Decide which pattern is additive, which has changing first differences but a stable second-difference pattern, and which is multiplicative.

xABC
0213
1546
28912
3111624
4142548
Answer / verification

A is additive/linear with first difference 3. B is quadratic with constant second differences. C is multiplicative/exponential with ratio 2.

Notes Example

Target: I can use tables, graphs, equations, and situations to distinguish additive, quadratic, and multiplicative patterns.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Classify the relationship as linear, quadratic, or exponential. Support your choice using differences or ratios.

xy
03
17
211
315
419
Answer / verification

U7_S4_NOTES_EXAMPLE_02Notes ExampleEquation
Target: I can formulate comparisons among linear, quadratic, and exponential functions using rates, differences, ratios, and graph features.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Classify $y=5(1.2)^x$ as linear, quadratic, or exponential. Identify the structural feature of the equation that supports your answer.

Answer / verification

Exponential; the variable appears in the exponent.

U7_S4_NOTES_EXAMPLE_03Notes ExampleEquation
Target: I can formulate comparisons among linear, quadratic, and exponential functions using rates, differences, ratios, and graph features.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

At $x=2$, compare the outputs of $L(x)=3x+1$, $Q(x)=x^2+1$, and $E(x)=2^x$. Which is greatest at this input? Then explain what additional pattern evidence would be needed to compare long-term growth.

Answer / verification

$L=7$, $Q=5$, and $E=4$, so the linear model is greatest at $x=2$. One input does not show the repeated pattern of differences/ratios or long-term behavior.

U7_S4_NOTES_EXAMPLE_04Notes ExampleContext
Target: I can critique claims about function families using evidence from multiple representations.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A table with y-values 5, 10, 15, 20 must be exponential because the outputs increase. Critique the claim using function-family evidence.

Answer / verification

The claim is not justified. The first difference is constant, so it is linear, not exponential.

U7_S4_NOTES_EXAMPLE_05Notes ExampleEquation + Table
Target: I can formulate comparisons among linear, quadratic, and exponential functions using rates, differences, ratios, and graph features.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Compare $L(x)=4x+1$ and $E(x)=2^x$ for nonnegative integer $x$. Find the first integer input where the exponential output is greater than the linear output. Show enough values to support your answer.

Answer / verification

The first such input is $x=5$: $E(5)=32$ and $L(5)=21$. Earlier nonnegative integers do not have $E(x)\gt L(x)$.

U7_S4_NOTES_EXAMPLE_06Notes ExampleContext
Target: I can use tables, graphs, equations, and situations to distinguish additive, quadratic, and multiplicative patterns.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

The area of a square is modeled from its side length. Which function family is the most natural model? Explain using the way the quantity changes.

Answer / verification

Quadratic; area depends on the square of side length.

Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

At $x=3$, compare the outputs of $L(x)=3x+1$, $Q(x)=x^2+1$, and $E(x)=2^x$. Which is greatest at this input? Then state why one input is insufficient to decide the long-run growth pattern.

Answer / verification

$L=10$, $Q=10$, and $E=8$, so the linear model is greatest at $x=3$. One input does not show the repeated pattern of differences/ratios or long-term behavior.

U7_S4_NOTES_YTI_04Notes YTIContext
Target: I can critique claims about function families using evidence from multiple representations.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A table with y-values 3, 9, 27, 81 is exponential because the ratio is constant. Critique the claim using function-family evidence.

Answer / verification

The claim is defensible. Each output is multiplied by 3.

U7_S4_NOTES_YTI_05Notes YTIEquation + Table
Target: I can formulate comparisons among linear, quadratic, and exponential functions using rates, differences, ratios, and graph features.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

The first such input is $x=4$: $E(4)=16$ and $L(4)=11$. Earlier nonnegative integers do not have $E(x)\gt L(x)$.

U7_S4_NOTES_YTI_06Notes YTIContext
Target: I can use tables, graphs, equations, and situations to distinguish additive, quadratic, and multiplicative patterns.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A population increases by 8% each year. Which function family is the most natural model? Explain using the way the quantity changes.

Answer / verification

Exponential; the same percent multiplier is applied repeatedly.

U7_S4_PRACTICE_SET_1_03Practice Set 1Graph
Target: I can use tables, graphs, equations, and situations to distinguish additive, quadratic, and multiplicative patterns.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Graphs A, B, C, and D show different function-family behavior. Identify the linear graph, the quadratic graph, and the two exponential graphs. For one choice, explain the graph feature you used.

Answer / verification

A is linear, B is quadratic, C is exponential growth, and D is exponential decay. For example, B has a turning point and parabola shape, while C and D show multiplicative curved growth/decay.

U7_S4_PRACTICE_SET_1_05Practice Set 1Equation
Target: I can formulate comparisons among linear, quadratic, and exponential functions using rates, differences, ratios, and graph features.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

At the single input $x=3$, compare $L(x)=3x+4$, $Q(x)=x^2+2$, and $E(x)=3^x$. Which has the greatest output at this input? Explain why this one-input comparison alone does not prove which family grows fastest in the long run.

Answer / verification

$L(3)=13$, $Q(3)=11$, and $E(3)=27$, so the exponential model is greatest at $x=3$. One input is local evidence only; long-run growth requires comparing behavior over increasing inputs.

U7_S4_PRACTICE_SET_1_06Practice Set 1Context
Target: I can critique claims about function families using evidence from multiple representations.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A parabola-shaped graph is enough by itself to prove a table has constant second differences. Critique the claim using function-family evidence.

Answer / verification

The claim is not justified. The representations should be connected with actual values; shape alone is not the full evidence.

U7_S4_PRACTICE_SET_1_08Practice Set 1Equation + Table

U7_S4_PRACTICE_SET_1_09Practice Set 1Context
Target: I can use tables, graphs, equations, and situations to distinguish additive, quadratic, and multiplicative patterns.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A ride service charges a starting fee of 5 dollars and then 3 dollars for each mile. Which function family best models total cost versus miles? Explain.

Answer / verification

Linear, because equal increases in miles add the same fixed amount to the cost.

U7_S4_PRACTICE_SET_1_18Practice Set 1Equation + Table
Target: I can formulate comparisons among linear, quadratic, and exponential functions using rates, differences, ratios, and graph features.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

For nonnegative integer inputs, compare $L(x)=3x+6$ and $E(x)=2(3)^x$. Find the first integer input at which the exponential model has the larger output. Show enough values to justify that it is the first.

Answer / verification

At $x=1$, $L=9$ and $E=6$; at $x=2$, $L=12$ and $E=18$. Therefore the first overtake occurs at $x=2$.

U7_S4_PRACTICE_SET_1_19Practice Set 1Equation
Target: Compare factoring, completing the square, and the quadratic formula and select efficient solution methods.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Choose an efficient method to solve $\displaystyle x^{2} - x - 6=0$, then solve. Briefly justify the method choice.

Answer / verification

Factoring is efficient for this structure. $x=3$ or $x=-2$.

U7_S4_PRACTICE_SET_1_20Practice Set 1Equation
Target: Compare factoring, completing the square, and the quadratic formula and select efficient solution methods.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

For $\displaystyle x^{2} + x - 6=0$, compare factoring with the quadratic formula. Which is more efficient here, and why? Then solve.

Answer / verification

Factoring is more efficient because the trinomial factors cleanly as $(x-2)(x+3)$. The solutions are $x=2$ and $x=-3$.

U7_S4_PRACTICE_SET_1_21Practice Set 1Equation
Target: Compare factoring, completing the square, and the quadratic formula and select efficient solution methods.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Inspect $\displaystyle 3 x^{2} - 9 x=0$. Identify the structural clue that suggests a fast solving method, then solve.

Answer / verification

A common factor of $x$ is visible, so factor: $x(3x-9)=0$. Thus $x=0$ or $x=3$.

U7_S4_PRACTICE_SET_1_22Practice Set 1Equation
Target: Compare standard, vertex, and factored forms of quadratic functions and determine which form best reveals key features.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

For $y=3x^2-4x+7$, name the quadratic form and state one key feature that this form reveals directly.

Answer / verification

Standard form; it directly reveals the y-intercept $\displaystyle 7$.

U7_S4_PRACTICE_SET_1_23Practice Set 1Equation
Target: Compare standard, vertex, and factored forms of quadratic functions and determine which form best reveals key features.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Rewrite $$x^2-5x+6$$ in factored form. Then state what feature becomes easier to read from the new form.

Answer / verification

$(x-2)(x-3)$. The zeros/x-intercepts are easier to read from factored form.

U7_S4_PRACTICE_SET_1_24Practice Set 1Context + Equation
Target: Compare standard, vertex, and factored forms of quadratic functions and determine which form best reveals key features.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A problem asks when a quadratic model reaches zero. Which quadratic form would you prefer to use first, and why?

Answer / verification

Factored Form, because the zeros are visible.

U7_S4_PRACTICE_SET_3_20Practice Set 3Equation
Target: Compare factoring, completing the square, and the quadratic formula and select efficient solution methods.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

For $\displaystyle x^{2} - 8 x + 11=0$, compare completing the square with the quadratic formula. Which is more efficient here, and why? Then solve.

Answer / verification

Completing the square is efficient because the quadratic is one square away from vertex form: $(x-4)^2=5$. Thus $x=4+\sqrt{5}$ or $x=4-\sqrt{5}$.

U7_S4_PRACTICE_SET_3_21Practice Set 3Equation

Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Inspect $\displaystyle 16 x^{2} - 16=0$. Identify the structural clue that suggests a fast solving method, then solve.

Answer / verification

This is a difference of squares: $(4x-4)(4x+4)=0$, so $x=\pm\frac{4}{4}$.

Compare $L(x)=5x+2$ and $E(x)=3^x$ for nonnegative integer $x$. Find the first integer input where the exponential output is greater than the linear output. Show enough values to support your answer.

Answer / verification

The first such input is $x=3$: $E(3)=27$ and $L(3)=17$. Earlier nonnegative integers do not have $E(x)\gt L(x)$.

U7_S4_EXTRA_PRACTICE_16Extra PracticeContext
Target: I can use tables, graphs, equations, and situations to distinguish additive, quadratic, and multiplicative patterns.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A ride service charges a starting fee of 5 dollars and then 5 dollars for each mile. Which function family best models total cost versus miles? Explain.

Answer / verification

Linear, because equal increases in miles add the same fixed amount to the cost.

U7_S4_EXTRA_PRACTICE_19Extra PracticeEquation
Target: Compare factoring, completing the square, and the quadratic formula and select efficient solution methods.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Choose an efficient method to solve $\displaystyle 3 x^{2} + 4 x - 1=0$, then solve. Briefly justify the method choice.

Answer / verification

Quadratic formula is efficient for this structure. $x=- \frac{2}{3} + \frac{\sqrt{7}}{3}$ or $x=- \frac{\sqrt{7}}{3} - \frac{2}{3}$.

U7_S4_EXTRA_PRACTICE_20Extra PracticeEquation
Target: Compare factoring, completing the square, and the quadratic formula and select efficient solution methods.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

For $\displaystyle x^{2} + x - 20=0$, compare factoring with the quadratic formula. Which is more efficient here, and why? Then solve.

Answer / verification

Factoring is more efficient because the trinomial factors cleanly as $(x-4)(x+5)$. The solutions are $x=4$ and $x=-5$.

U7_S4_EXTRA_PRACTICE_21Extra PracticeEquation
Target: Compare factoring, completing the square, and the quadratic formula and select efficient solution methods.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Inspect $\displaystyle x^{2} + 10 x + 25=0$. Identify the structural clue that suggests a fast solving method, then solve.

Answer / verification

This is a perfect-square trinomial: $(x+5)^2=0$, so $x=-5$.

$f(x)=(x+3)(x-4)$; zeros $x=-3$ and $x=4$.

Vertex form, because the vertex coordinates are visible directly in $a(x-h)^2+k$.

U7_S4_WARM_UP_1_02Warm-Up 1Equation

Answer / verification

An efficient choice is quadratic formula. The solution is $x=\frac{-1\pm\sqrt{33}}{4}$.

U7_S4_WARM_UP_1_03Warm-Up 1Equation
Target: Compare factoring, completing the square, and the quadratic formula and select efficient solution methods.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

For $x^2-2x-7=0$, compare two possible solving methods and choose the more efficient one. Then solve.

Answer / verification

Completing the square or quadratic formula avoids searching for integer factors. The solution is $x=1\pm2\sqrt2$.

U7_S4_WARM_UP_1_04Warm-Up 1Equation

Answer / verification

Choose factoring out $\displaystyle 3x$. The solutions are $x=0,4$.

Warm-Up 3

U7_S4_WARM_UP_3_01Warm-Up 3Graph
Target: I can use tables, graphs, equations, and situations to distinguish additive, quadratic, and multiplicative patterns.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Graphs A, B, C, and D show different function-family behavior. Identify the linear graph, the quadratic graph, and the two exponential graphs. For one choice, explain the graph feature you used.

Answer / verification

A is linear, B is quadratic, C is exponential growth, and D is exponential decay. For example, B has a turning point and parabola shape, while C and D show multiplicative curved growth/decay.

U7_S4_WARM_UP_3_02Warm-Up 3Equation
Target: Compare factoring, completing the square, and the quadratic formula and select efficient solution methods.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

For $\displaystyle x^{2} - 12 x + 29=0$, compare completing the square with the quadratic formula. Which is more efficient here, and why? Then solve.

Answer / verification

Completing the square is efficient because the quadratic is one square away from vertex form: $(x-6)^2=7$. Thus $x=6+\sqrt{7}$ or $x=6-\sqrt{7}$.

U7_S4_WARM_UP_3_03Warm-Up 3Equation
Target: Compare standard, vertex, and factored forms of quadratic functions and determine which form best reveals key features.
Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

Rewrite $$x^2+6x+5$$ in factored form. Then state what feature becomes easier to read from the new form.

Answer / verification

$(x+1)(x+5)$. The zeros/x-intercepts are easier to read from factored form.

U7_S4_WARM_UP_3_04Warm-Up 3Context + Equation

Evidence job: Instruction/practice aligned to the exact target.

A problem asks for the starting value $f(0)$. Which quadratic form would you prefer to use first, and why?

Answer / verification

Standard Form, because the constant term gives the y-intercept directly.

7.5 Exponential Modeling with Data

Students will create and analyze exponential models using real-world data.

Answer / verification

The successive ratios are roughly 1.2, so an exponential growth model is plausible even though the ratios are not identical. Measurement variability or changing real-world conditions could cause the departures.

U7_S5_NOTES_EXAMPLE_04Notes ExampleTable + Context

U7_S5_NOTES_EXAMPLE_05Notes ExampleEquation + Context

Answer / verification

The leading value 40 is the modeled value at $t=0$. The factor represents a 50% increase per period.

The leading value 900 is the modeled value at $t=0$. The factor represents an 18% decrease per period.

U7_SUM_SUMMATIVE_V1_02Summative V1Table + Equation
Target: Factor polynomial expressions by identifying a greatest common factor and use multiplication to verify equivalent forms.
Evidence job: Summative evidence for U7-MG01: Factor polynomial expressions by identifying a greatest common factor and use multiplication to verify equivalent forms.

Use the term-structure table to identify the greatest common factor. Then factor $8x^2+20x$ completely and verify the factored form by multiplication.

termcoefficientpower of $x$
$8x^2$82
$20x$201
Answer / verification

The graph below represents $H(t)=-(t-1)(t-7)$. Identify both zeros and explain how the factors correspond to the horizontal intercepts shown on the graph.

Answer / verification

The zeros are $t=1$ and $t=7$, corresponding to horizontal intercepts $(1,0)$ and $(7,0)$. Each factor becomes zero at one intercept input.

U7_SUM_SUMMATIVE_V6_07Summative V6Equation
Target: Connect factored form, factors, zeros, x-intercepts, and quadratic graph behavior.
Evidence job: Summative evidence for U7-MG03: Connect factored form, factors, zeros, x-intercepts, and quadratic graph behavior.

Compare $f(x)=(x+2)(x-5)$ and $g(x)=4(x+2)(x-5)$. Explain what happens to their zeros and $x$-intercepts when the factor 4 is introduced.

Answer / verification

The zeros remain $-2$ and $\displaystyle 5$, so the $x$-intercepts are unchanged. The vertical scale changes, not the roots.

U7_SUM_SUMMATIVE_V6_08Summative V6Context + Equation
Target: Compare standard, vertex, and factored forms and choose the form that most directly reveals the feature needed for a problem or context.
Evidence job: Summative evidence for U7-MG04: Compare standard, vertex, and factored forms and choose the form that most directly reveals the feature needed for a problem or context.

A student says, “Standard form is always the best form because it is the usual way to write a quadratic.” Critique the claim with one situation where vertex form or factored form is more efficient.

Answer / verification

The claim is false. Vertex form is more efficient for a maximum/minimum or vertex; factored form is more efficient for zeros/intercepts.

U7_SUM_SUMMATIVE_V6_09Summative V6Equation
Target: Compare standard, vertex, and factored forms and choose the form that most directly reveals the feature needed for a problem or context.
Evidence job: Summative evidence for U7-MG04: Compare standard, vertex, and factored forms and choose the form that most directly reveals the feature needed for a problem or context.

Show that $y=(x-3)^2-4$ and $y=x^2-6x+5$ are equivalent by expanding. Then state one feature that is easier to see in each form.

Answer / verification

Expanding gives $x^2-6x+9-4=x^2-6x+5$. Vertex form reveals $(3,-4)$; standard form reveals the $y$-intercept 5.

U7_SUM_SUMMATIVE_V6_10Summative V6Equation
Target: Compare standard, vertex, and factored forms and choose the form that most directly reveals the feature needed for a problem or context.
Evidence job: Summative evidence for U7-MG04: Compare standard, vertex, and factored forms and choose the form that most directly reveals the feature needed for a problem or context.

A quadratic is $q(x)=(x+1)(x-9)$. The next question asks for its zeros, then its axis of symmetry. Use the factored form to find the zeros and reason from them to the axis of symmetry.

Answer / verification

The zeros are $-1$ and $\displaystyle 9$. Their midpoint is $\displaystyle 4$, so the axis of symmetry is $x=4$.
